### [A Scalable Predictive Modeling Approach to Duplicate Detection in Pharmacovigilance: Algorithm Development and Validation Study](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e95282/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-10-09` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `67` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e95282/`

&lt;strong&gt;Background:&lt;/strong&gt; Adverse event reports referring to the same case but treated as independent can negatively impact statistical analysis and may mislead clinical assessment. Pharmacovigilance relies on large databases of adverse event reports to discover potential new causal associations. Their size necessitates computational methods to identify duplicates at scale. The current state-of-the-art is statistical record linkage which outperforms rule-based approaches. In particular, vigiMatch is in routine use for VigiBase, the World Health Organization global database of adverse event reports, and represents the first statistical duplicate detection approach in pharmacovigilance deployed at scale. Originally developed for both drugs and vaccines, its application to vaccines has been limited due to inconsistent performance across countries. &lt;strong&gt;Objective:&lt;/strong&gt; This study aims to advance the state-of-the-art for duplicate detection in large-scale pharmacovigilance databases and achieve more consistent performance across adverse event reports from different countries. &lt;strong&gt;Methods:&lt;/strong&gt; This paper extends vigiMatch from probabilistic record linkage to predictive modeling, refining features for drugs, vaccines, and adverse events using country-specific reporting rates, extracting dates from free text, and training separate support vector machine classifiers for drugs and vaccines. Recall was evaluated using 5 independent reference sets. Precision was assessed by annotating random selections of report pairs classified as duplicates. &lt;strong&gt;Results:&lt;/strong&gt; Precision for the new method was 92% for vaccines and 54% for drugs, compared with 41% for a previous generation method. Recall ranged from 80% to 85% across test sets for vaccines and from 40% to 86% for drugs, compared with 24%-53% for the comparator method. &lt;strong&gt;Conclusions:&lt;/strong&gt; Predictive modeling, use of free text, and country-specific features advance state-of-the-art for duplicate detection in pharmacovigilance. &lt;strong&gt;Trial Registration:&lt;/strong&gt; <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/a9cc9151c043e5782c148d0197e4de78" />

### [AI Model Reporting Standards in Health Care: Systematic Review and Consolidation](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e92329/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-10-09` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `51` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e92329/`

Background: AI technologies are currently experiencing significant growth in development and use in health care. The AI model lifecycle includes several stages, all of which should be adequately described. Reporting standards are commonly used for this purpose. However, the plain-text structure of these standards prevents transparent machine-readability of this information. Objective: This systematic review aims to investigate AI model reporting standards from clinical databases and technical documents published since 2016. Methods: The methodological systematic review was conducted according to the PRISMA (Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses) 2020 guidelines. The inclusion criteria of this systematic review were papers published from 2016 to April 2026 in English and in a peer-reviewed journal with open access. We aimed to include standards for different data types (structured, images, and text-based). The exclusion criteria were specific medical domain, incomplete reports, and reviews. The following libraries have been consulted: Scopus and Web of Science, MEDLINE and Embase, the IEEE Xplore digital library, and the ACM Digital Library. We applied an Automated Systematic Review software tool (Utrecht University) to find relevant papers after records retrieval. Two reviewers (EA, DS) screened relevant papers independently and invited a third reviewer (JvS) to reach a consensus. All included papers were analyzed to extract relevant lifecycle stage, publication type, data type, and reporting questions. The results were presented and synthesized in a consolidated list by grouping questions and ordering them in a logical sequence according to the AI model lifecycle. Results: The total number of included studies was 25 from clinical and technical databases (22 and 3, respectively). The consolidated list includes 134 questions ordered in 7 groups: “Problem identification,” “Data collection and processing,” “Model development,” “Technical validation,” “Functional validation,” “Operation &amp; Monitoring,” and one additional group with “Statements.” Groups according to the lifecycle stages contain all relevant information for each stage. Both clinical and technical papers reflect information for “Problem identification” (s=5), “Data collection and processing” (s=25), “Model development” (s=13), “Technical validation” (s=14). While “Functional validation” (s=30) was represented mostly in clinical papers and “Operation &amp; Monitoring” (s=18) in technical ones. In the “Statements” (s=29), all general questions were summarized, describing reusability and reproducibility of the model, and highlighting ethical considerations, such as model fairness, explainability, interpretability, and related risk. It was shown that only 10 out of 15 FAIR (findable, accessible, interoperable, reusable) principles were addressed in the consolidated list, with focus on providing rich metadata (F2) and accurate and relevant descriptions (R1). Conclusions: We furthermore considered the mapping of the consolidated questions with FAIR principles. In future, we aim to incorporate the results of our work into a metadata schema for AI models developed according to FAIR principles. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/a231726077262efcf22a50ba77fa703a" />

### [Exploring College Students’ Compliance During a 60-Day Alcohol-Related Ecological Momentary Assessment Using Location- and Time-Based Questions: Prospective Observational Study](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e81713)

- Source: `jmir_mhealth` · published `2026-10-09` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `54` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e81713`

<strong>Background:</strong> Ecological momentary assessment (EMA) methods can provide assessment of alcohol-related beliefs and behaviors when people are in their natural environments. An increasingly common EMA approach involves the use of passive sensors (eg, continuous sharing of Bluetooth and GPS cell phone data) to collect rich data and trigger alcohol assessment in specific contexts. While collecting real-time assessments allows researchers to explore contextual factors impacting alcohol use, there is a lack of research examining compliance rates to alcohol-related EMAs that use daily questions for study periods over 2 weeks. EMAs over longer periods allow researchers to assess meaningful changes over time and sample low-base-rate events. Further, there is limited research exploring how EMA compliance is influenced by individual-level factors (eg, privacy concerns and alcohol use). Identifying factors associated with compliance can help inform EMA protocol design. <strong>Objective:</strong> This study had 2 primary goals. First, we add to the literature by examining the feasibility and acceptability of a 60-day college student alcohol-EMA that uses phone-based, passive sensors. Second, we examined whether compliance was associated with event-level alcohol use, privacy concerns, and types of questions asked (ie, questions triggered by time or GPS location). <strong>Methods:</strong> College students (N=68) who reported regular alcohol use completed the 60-day EMA study. At baseline, participants completed an online survey that included an assessment of privacy concerns. During the study period, participants completed up to 15 questions a day, including evening and morning self-reports of alcohol use. Feasibility was assessed based on enrollment, retention, and compliance rates. Acceptability was examined at the end of the study period using open- and close-answered questions on perceptions of the EMA experience. <strong>Results:</strong> The findings supported the feasibility of the 60-day EMA with enrollment exceeding benchmarks and high compliance across the study period (average compliance=90%). Overall, students found the protocol acceptable with 88% (58/66) indicating that they would be willing to complete another EMA study. Participants’ compliance was not associated with alcohol use at the event-level (odds ratio [OR] 0.94, 95% CI 0.80-1.10) or at the trait level (OR 0.92, 95% CI 0.73-1.17). Participants’ compliance was greater for time-trigger assessment than GPS-triggered questions (<i>P</i>&lt;.001). Privacy concerns were not associated with reduced responding. <strong>Conclusions:</strong> This study has implications for those designing alcohol EMAs for college populations that involve the use of passive sensors, including recommendations for balancing predictable timing of questions and variability in question content.

### [How People Integrate Mental Health Apps Into Everyday Self-Care: Qualitative Study](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e95414)

- Source: `jmir_mhealth` · published `2026-10-09` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `54` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e95414`

Background: Mobile mental health (MH) apps are increasingly promoted as tools for self-care. However, less is known about how users integrate them into everyday life and how they combine with other forms of support. Objective: This qualitative study examined what motivates individuals to adopt MH apps and how these tools are used alongside other care practices. Methods: We conducted semistructured interviews with 31 Czech MH app users aged 18‐56 (23 women), recruited through social media, researchers’ networks, and snowball sampling. Eligible participants had used an MH app for at least 2 weeks; individuals currently experiencing a psychological crisis were excluded. Interviews were conducted online or face-to-face and analyzed using reflexive thematic analysis by Braun and Clarke. The analysis was inductive and informed by a critical realist epistemological stance. Initial analytic engagement was conducted independently, followed by collaborative theme development and refinement. Results: We identified the following three motivational patterns for MH app use: (1) regular use to maintain well-being and support preventive self-care; (2) reactive use during periods of acute distress; and (3) temporary, problem-focused use aimed at understanding or addressing a specific difficulty. Beyond motivations for adoption, our findings revealed the following four distinct trajectories of app engagement and disengagement: (1) emancipation, with apps acting as ongoing support for self-care when therapy is reduced or ended; (2) independence, as techniques learned through apps are transferred into everyday life and used without the app; (3) reliance, where users persist with app-based self-care despite insufficient benefit, occasionally delaying or limiting other forms of help; and (4) episodic use, characterized by intermittent engagement during specific life periods. These findings extend existing research by showing that disengagement is not necessarily a marker of failure but can reflect successful self-care and the transfer of learned skills. We also identified tensions and unintended consequences, including the burden of sustaining engagement, risks of overreliance, emotional costs associated with sustained self-monitoring, and tensions between digital self-care and efforts to reduce screen use. Participants generally viewed self-monitoring as beneficial when guided by clear personal goals and embedded within a broader system of self-care. Conclusions: Our findings suggest that appropriate disengagement can signal a successful step in self-care. App design and policy should acknowledge episodic use and strengthen connections to human support with clear guidance and accessible pathways to additional care when needed.

### [Multilingual Disparities in Large Language Model–Based Symptom Detection for Global Disease Surveillance: Evaluation Study](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e103321/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-10-09` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `55` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e103321/`

Background: Symptom detection is essential in global disease surveillance to detect potential outbreaks, as symptoms are the first observable signs of infection. To reflect real-time ground truth conditions during pandemics, social media has emerged as a valuable data source. Moreover, effective digital disease surveillance systems must operate across diverse linguistic settings, and large language models (LLMs) have been shown to perform inconsistently across languages, tending to have lower performance in low-resource languages. While multilingual approaches have been explored in various health-related natural language processing tasks, a critical gap remains in understanding whether LLM-based symptom detection can perform consistently across languages for global disease surveillance. Southeast Asia demonstrates this challenge, combining diverse languages and the potential for emerging infectious disease outbreaks, making it a case for evaluating how multilingual performance disparities manifest in symptom detection. Objective: This study aims to evaluate multilingual disparities in symptom detection using a LLM, as well as the associated error mechanisms across languages and symptom types, to better understand their implications for global disease surveillance. Methods: This study uses the MedWeb dataset, a multilingual pseudo–social media text dataset with multiple symptom labels. The data consist of 12 languages, covering diverse regions and language resource classifications. The symptoms included in this study are fever, headache, runny nose, cough, diarrhea, hay fever, influenza, and cold. We used GPT-5 as the symptom detection system, representing a strong model for health-related tasks. The results are evaluated using the macro precision, recall, and -score. An error analysis was conducted to identify the error mechanisms underlying incorrect symptom predictions across languages and to calculate the impact of each error category. Results: Our findings show that performance varied across languages, language resource groups, and symptom types. Southeast Asian (SEA) languages generally achieved lower scores than non-SEA languages, with Japanese obtaining the highest -score (0.812) and Lao the lowest (0.716). High-resource languages achieved the most consistent performance, while low-resource languages obtained the lowest overall scores. At the symptom level, diarrhea, headache, cough, and fever showed stable detection across languages, while hay fever, runny nose, influenza, and cold exhibited greater variability. Hay fever showed the widest variability, forming 2 distinct performance clusters aligned with language resource and region classifications. The error analysis revealed 4 misclassification patterns: explicitly mentioned symptoms, cross-lingual variation, symptom overgeneralization, and context misinterpretation. Cross-lingual variation was the most frequent error category, while errors related to explicitly mentioned symptoms showed the largest potential impact on model performance. Conclusions: Due to performance disparities, achieving more reliable and equitable LLM-based symptom detection from social media text for global disease surveillance would benefit from broader representation in training data for low-resource languages, improved cultural and linguistic sensitivity, and stronger contextual understanding of symptom-related expressions. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/0c930742221c409e5f2b4421f997ec18" />

### [Stakeholder Perspectives on Digital Health Technologies in Poststroke Self-Management and Rehabilitation: Systematic Review Based on the NASSS Framework](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e95197)

- Source: `jmir_mhealth` · published `2026-10-09` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `60` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e95197`

Background: Digital health technologies (DHTs) offer opportunities to support poststroke self-management and rehabilitation, but their implementation may be hindered by mismatches between technological design, stakeholder needs, and care contexts. Synthesizing stakeholder perspectives can clarify implementation priorities and gaps in the evidence. Objective: This study aimed to synthesize stakeholders’ perceptions, experiences, and recommendations regarding DHT implementation in poststroke self-management and rehabilitation and to map the findings to the NASSS (Nonadoption, Abandonment, Scale-Up, Spread, and Sustainability) framework. Methods: Six databases (PubMed, MEDLINE, Embase, Scopus, CINAHL, and Web of Science) were searched from inception through October 2025. Eligible studies were English-language, peer-reviewed primary studies using qualitative, quantitative, or mixed methods designs to report stakeholder perspectives on DHTs in poststroke care. Two reviewers independently screened studies, extracted data, and assessed methodological quality using the Mixed Methods Appraisal Tool (MMAT). A convergent integrated synthesis was conducted: quantitative findings were transformed into textual descriptors, combined with qualitative findings, coded inductively, and subsequently mapped deductively to NASSS domains and subdomains. Disagreements were resolved through consensus or by third-reviewer adjudication. Results: Of 15,262 records identified, 42 studies published between 2019 and 2025 were included: 24 qualitative, 8 quantitative, and 10 mixed methods studies. Patients, health care professionals, caregivers, and members of the public were represented in 32, 19, 8, and 3 studies, respectively, with some studies including multiple groups. Evidence concentrated on the condition, technology, value proposition, and adopter-system domains. Stakeholders valued personalized functions, intuitive interfaces, timely feedback, and digital support that complemented human care. Barriers included stroke-related motor, cognitive, communication, and sensory impairments; unequal digital access and literacy; caregiver demands; staff workload and training needs; and poor integration with clinical routines. Data privacy and unclear payment arrangements also raised concerns. Evidence on organizational readiness and wider-system determinants was limited, and no eligible study directly examined interaction and adaptation over time. Methodological concerns included nonresponse bias and insufficient interpretation of integrated findings in mixed methods studies. Conclusions: Stakeholder perspectives indicate that DHT implementation in poststroke care should combine accessible, personalized design with support for patients, caregivers, and health care professionals, alongside integration into clinical workflows and clear data governance and financing arrangements. The NASSS mapping identified priorities beyond usability, but current evidence does not establish sustained adoption, scale-up, or spread. Longitudinal studies in routine care are needed to evaluate adaptation and sustainability.

### [Use of Generic Large Language Model Chatbots for Mental Health Issues Among Patients Seeking Psychotherapy: Cross-Sectional Mixed Methods Analysis in a German University Hospital Outpatient Clinic](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e104297/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-10-09` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `60` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e104297/`

&lt;strong&gt;Background:&lt;/strong&gt; Large language model–based chatbots (LLM-CBs) are increasingly used as mental health support tools. Risks and harms are discussed especially for unsupervised use and for vulnerable groups. A naturalistic characterization of the use of LLM-CBs by patients with mental health disorders is currently lacking. &lt;strong&gt;Objective:&lt;/strong&gt; The goal of this cross-sectional study is to investigate demographic and clinical characteristics of users of LLM-CB for mental health and their associations with use behavior. &lt;strong&gt;Methods:&lt;/strong&gt; Across 6 months, qualitative and quantitative data on LLM-CB use for mental health were collected in a consecutive sample of psychotherapy outpatients in routine treatment with at least 1 diagnosed mental health disorder. Results are reported descriptively and supported by exploratory inferential analyses. &lt;strong&gt;Results:&lt;/strong&gt; Within a total of 812 included outpatients who reported on LLM-CB use for mental health, 245 (30.1%) reported having used LLM-CBs for mental health (once: 69/217, 31.8%; weekly: 118/217, 54.4%; and daily: 30/217, 13.8%). Users were more likely to be younger, female, better educated, students, or unemployed. In total, 61.4% (121/197) have used LLM-CBs in self-reported personal crisis situations, of whom, 67.7% (82/121) found the experience unhelpful. LLM-CB use showed a weak, imprecise association with the number of diagnoses in the included diagnosis groups and was higher for anxiety and eating disorder diagnoses and lower for somatoform disorders. Post hoc multivariable analyses suggest that higher use in eating disorders and lower use in somatoform disorders were attenuated after adjusting for age, while higher use for anxiety disorders persisted. LLM-CBs were generally evaluated positively across various dimensions, especially when self-reported personal crisis situation use was experienced as helpful. Qualitative analysis of an open-ended question on use experience shows LLM-CB use across 4 domains reported in the selected subsample: general psychological assistance, disorder-specific assistance, information assistance, and everyday assistance. &lt;strong&gt;Conclusions:&lt;/strong&gt; This naturalistic characterization of LLM-CB use in mental health outpatients implies that harms and benefits of LLM-CB use are associated with demographic and clinical profiles. The results suggest that asking about LLM-CB use may be a useful consideration in psychosocial assessment, particularly regarding crisis use, symptom-reinforcing use, and replacement of human support. &lt;strong&gt;Trial Registration:&lt;/strong&gt; <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/cf52d240b74e6538165cfa03d977d64a" />

### [Interpretable Machine Learning–Derived Nomogram Model to Predict Population Health Literacy: Cross-Sectional Development and Validation Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e77654)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-13` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `55` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e77654`

Background: Health literacy (HL) is a major factor associated with population health. However, no visualized predictive model has been developed to identify populations with low HL in a cost-effective manner. Objective: This study aims to identify key determinants of HL and develop a predictive nomogram model for population-level HL screening. Methods: This study analyzed cross-sectional data from 56,340 residents (aged 15‐69 y) in Zhejiang Province (2023) And used the Chinese Health Literacy Scale to measure HL. We used 3 complementary analytical approaches: logistic regression (LR) analysis to identify HL-associated factors, machine learning models (gradient boosting decision tree [GBDT], extreme gradient boosting [XGBoost], LR, and random forest [RF]) with Shapley additive explanations (SHAP) to quantify predictor importance, and nomogram development using 2023 data with external validation on 2024 data. In the nomogram, model 1 used all the predictive variables, while model 2 used the 5 core predictive variables determined through SHAP analysis. Model performance was evaluated using receiver operating characteristic curves, calibration plots, and decision curve analysis. Results: The nomogram models designed for predicting HL demonstrated robust stability, with good results in external validation. The area under the curve (AUC) values for both the test and validation sets of the machine learning models exceeded 0.75. At the 0.5 threshold, the sensitivities of the GBDT, XGBoost, LR, and RF test sets and validation sets were all >60%. The specificity of the test sets and validation sets of all 4 machine learning models was >75%. The positive predictive value (PPV) and negative predictive value (NPV) were both >60%. The AUC value of nomogram model 1 developed using the data from 2023 was 0.821, and the AUC value of the externally validated nomogram model 1 was 0.814. Similarly, nomogram model 2 had AUC values of 0.819 in the internal validation and 0.812 in the external validation. The PPV and NPV of both the internal validation model 1 and model 2 were all >60%. The integration of GBDT, XGBoost, LR, and RF models, along with the SHAP method, highlighted education level, age, annual household income, smoking, and occupation as the 5 most critical determinants. Conclusions: By using the GBDT, XGBoost, LR, RF, and nomogram models, a predictive model with substantial efficacy was effectively developed and validated for forecasting the HL levels of the population. This comprehensive model not only exhibits a commendable predictive capacity but also identifies the pivotal factors influencing HL. Such insights are crucial for shaping health policies and fostering healthier behaviors across the population.

### [Predicting Measles Vaccination Defaulting in Uganda and Kenya: Comparative Evaluation of Custom Soft Voting and TabNet Stacked Ensemble Architectures](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e95727/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-10-13` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `53` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e95727/`

Background: Measles is a major cause of death among infants despite free routine immunization (RI) in low- and middle-income countries (LMICs). Vaccination defaulting contributes to measles outbreak occurrences. Conventional immunization defaulter identification methods are expensive to implement, prone to data falsification, reactive, and overestimate uptake. Several machine learning studies leveraged the use of child and maternal characteristics as predictors of immunization defaulting, overlooking community, demographic, and socioeconomic factors. Similarly, there is limited research regarding the use of interpretable deep learning and advanced ensemble methods to improve model performance in predicting RI defaulters and establishing significant predictors at the national level in the LMIC context, like Uganda. Objective: This study predicts infants at a risk of defaulting on measles vaccination in Uganda and Kenya using advanced ensemble learning architectures. Methods: The study benchmarked classical and deep learning models using Demographic and Health Survey (DHS) datasets (2016-2022) with child, maternal, community, social-economic, and demographic characteristics as predictors. The robust modeling pipeline began with feature selection according to the literature and the expert domain. It was followed by vaccination eligibility, missing value handling, and feature elimination using the phi-k correlation framework. To mitigate class imbalance, synthetic minority over-sampling technique for nominal continuous features was used in a training pipeline after the RobustScaler. Nine baseline classifiers, including Tabular Network (TabNet), multilayer perceptron (MLP), random forest (RF), gradient boosting (GB), adaptive-boosting (AB), among others, underwent stratified k-fold cross-validation and hyperparameter optimization via grid search. Custom soft voting was evaluated alongside TabNet stack architecture for superior ensemble prediction. Models were evaluated for accuracy, precision, recall, and -score. Model explainability was decoded using Shapley additive explanations (SHAP), while Streamlit and SQLite formed the tool architecture. Results: The proposed TabNet+GB+RF custom soft voting achieved significant, nonrandom performance over tuned base models with an -score of 0.7111, area under the curve (AUC) of 0.8847 for Kenya. Conversely, for Uganda, the TabNet hybrid ensemble (GB+RF+ AB→TabNet) was the best with AUC of 0.8862 and -score of 0.6756, which was statistically insignificant. SHAP identified child’s age and vitamin A1 as universal key predictors in both countries; others varied. In Kenya, time to water source, respondents’ education, district lived previously, and current district were the most influential global features, respectively. In Uganda, Polio inactive, Pentavalent3, current district, and respondents’ education were the most influential factors to defaulting, respectively. Conclusions: The proposed advanced ensemble learning models improved the classification of measles vaccine defaulters compared to individual models. The study recommends distinct country-tailored strategies. In Kenya, outreaches are recommended to areas with low literacy, distant water sources, and mobile populations. Conversely, in Uganda, the study recommends reminders to avoid missing preceding vaccines and outreaches to high-default districts. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/1ca8a17e6c670ca53851a5e7495c815c" />

### [Support-Seeking After Market Withdrawal of a Purpose-Built AI Well-Being Tool (Ash): Cross-Sectional Survey](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e96515)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-13` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `54` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e96515`

<strong>Background:</strong> Purpose-built AI well-being tools—designed with clinical safeguards for everyday emotional difficulties such as stress, grief, and relationship conflict—represent a growing category of digital mental health support. When such tools are removed from the market, users must navigate alternatives on their own; however, no empirical data exist on where they actually turn. <strong>Objective:</strong> We aimed to characterize real-world support-seeking after the withdrawal of a purpose-built AI well-being mobile app (Ash), specifically to (1) document where former users turned for emotional and mental health support, (2) characterize how general-purpose AI tools were used for mental health, (3) assess perceived changes in service access and subjective well-being, and (4) capture qualitative experiences of the transition. <strong>Methods:</strong> We conducted a cross-sectional survey of 393 UK-based adults who had used Ash within 90 days before its withdrawal from the UK market in January 2026. Of 7415 randomly sampled eligible users invited by email, 393 (response rate 5.3%) responded and 364 (92.6%) completed all items. The survey assessed self-reported sources of emotional and mental health support following withdrawal, use of general-purpose AI tools, perceived changes in access to support, and subjective well-being. Free-text responses (n=272) were analyzed using codebook-based qualitative content analysis, with the codebook developed through a hybrid inductive-deductive process. Reporting follows the CHERRIES (Checklist for Reporting Results of Internet E-Surveys) guidelines. <strong>Results:</strong> The most common primary sources of mental health support since losing access to a purpose-built AI well-being application were general-purpose AI tools (27.1%, 104/384; 95% CI 22.9-31.7) and no support at all (26%, 100/384; 95% CI 21.9-30.7). Relatively fewer respondents reported turning to National Health Service services (19/384, 4.9%; 95% CI 3.2-7.6) or a private therapist (8.1%, 31/384; 95% CI 5.7-11.2) as their primary resource. Among the 157 respondents reporting use of general-purpose AI for mental health support, the dominant motivations were instant access (78.3%, 123/157) and cost (73.9%, 116/157) rather than perceived helpfulness (18.5%, 29/157). Overall, 60.2% (219/364; 95% CI 55.1-65.1) reported worse access to support, rising to 80.7% (71/88; 95% CI 71.2-87.6) among those who had not sought support since. Qualitative themes converged on these patterns: 33.8% (92/272) reported finding no suitable alternative, 22.8% (62/272) judged general-purpose AI inferior, and 15.8% (43/272) expressed emotional distress at the loss. <strong>Conclusions:</strong> In this exploratory, retrospective survey, removing a purpose-built AI well-being tool was not associated with a redirection to human-led mental health services; most respondents turned to general-purpose AI or stopped seeking support entirely, and those who disengaged reported the poorest well-being. Regulatory frameworks should account for real-world care-seeking behavior when evaluating the risks and benefits of purpose-built AI tools for general wellness. <strong>Trial Registration:</strong>

### [Tablet-Based Neurocognitive Tests for Ugandan Adolescents Living With HIV: Cross-Sectional Validation Study](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e103288)

- Source: `jmir_mhealth` · published `2026-10-13` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `63` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e103288`

Background: Adolescents living with perinatally acquired HIV (PHIV) remain at risk of neurocognitive difficulties, yet access to valid neurocognitive tests is limited in sub-Saharan Africa (SSA), where most of this population resides. Tests that can be administered by nonspecialist personnel are culturally and linguistically appropriate for SSA’s diverse populations and are brief enough for routine use are needed to expand access, but few such tools exist. Objective: We adapted , a brief tablet-based neurocognitive test battery, into Luganda and evaluated its validity and acceptability for use among Ugandan adolescents with PHIV. Methods: In this cross-sectional validation study, 100 Ugandan adolescents aged 11 to 20 years (50 with PHIV; 50 HIV-negative controls) completed the Luganda- or English-language battery and a commonly used traditional test battery. Participants were recruited from an existing longitudinal cardiovascular cohort at the Joint Clinical Research Centre in Kampala, Uganda. We examined convergent validity with correlations between the 2 batteries’ individual and global -scores, known-groups validity with unadjusted and age-adjusted group comparisons, criterion validity by examining classification agreement between the 2 batteries quantified with Cohen κ, and acceptability through self-reported technology use and ease of use. Results: and the traditional battery global -scores were strongly correlated (=0.75; <.001). Correlations between tests assessing similar domains were generally small to moderate, and were strongest for processing speed, attention, and working memory. Adolescents with PHIV scored significantly lower than controls on global scores for both (=.008) and the traditional battery (=.003), even after adjusting for age. Agreement between batteries in classifying participants ≤1 SD (κ=0.56) and ≤2 SD (κ=0.66) below the global -score mean was moderate, with both batteries classifying more adolescents with PHIV than controls below these thresholds. Although fewer than half of participants (42/97, 43%) had previously used a tablet, most rated as easy to use, and prior tablet experience was not independently associated with performance after age adjustment. Conclusions: The Luganda-adapted demonstrated convergent, known-groups, and criterion validity in Ugandan adolescents with PHIV. By delivering automated administration, automated scoring, and standardized multimedia instructions in the local language on a portable tablet, addresses several persistent barriers to neurocognitive assessment in low-resource settings, including shortages of specialist personnel, the time burden of traditional batteries, and the scarcity of culturally and linguistically appropriate tools. The battery was found to be highly acceptable among adolescents with little or no prior tablet experience. These findings add to a growing body of evidence supporting tablet-based assessment as a practical and scalable mobile health approach to expanding access to neurocognitive testing. Future research should evaluate test-retest reliability, sensitivity to change over time, normative benchmarks, and implementation within real-world clinical workflows.

Agilent’s deal comes months after Roche agreed to buy PathAI for up to $1.05 billion. Evercore ISI analysts noted that Agilent “got the rights to play in this market at a lower price point.”

Siemens Healthineers said Monday that it will no longer sell general imaging ultrasound systems, focusing instead on ultrasound used in procedural imaging. The company will focus on systems used in interventional cardiology and electrophysiology procedures. Siemens Healthineers said this approach will allow it to bring together its imaging systems, software capabilities and partnerships with heart device companies to support physicians in increasingly complex procedures. Siemens Healthineers said it will continue to provide support for its general ultrasound systems, including maintenance, software updates and transducer availability.

### [A Machine Learning Framework for Predicting Functional Status and Estimating Health Care Costs in Pulmonary Arterial Hypertension: Retrospective Cohort Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e97372)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-15` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `45` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e97372`

<strong>Background:</strong> Pulmonary arterial hypertension (PAH) is a rare and progressive disease associated with substantial clinical heterogeneity and high health care costs. While machine learning (ML) has been increasingly applied to predict clinical outcomes, approaches that integrate clinical prediction with downstream economic modeling remain limited. <strong>Objective:</strong> This study proposes and evaluates an ML-based framework that combines prediction of future functional status with estimation of associated health care costs in patients with PAH. <strong>Methods:</strong> In this retrospective cohort study, data from 181 patients with PAH were analyzed. A total of 6 supervised ML algorithms (Least Absolute Shrinkage and Selection Operator [LASSO] regression, ridge regression, k-nearest neighbors, decision trees, random forest, and gradient boosting) were trained to predict the relative change in World Health Organization functional class (WHO-FC) from baseline to 12-month follow-up using baseline clinical variables. Predicted change rates were subsequently converted to predicted WHO-FC values at the 12-month follow-up for downstream health care cost estimation. Missing values in predictor variables were imputed using median substitution. Median imputation was performed before the dataset was split into training and test sets. Model performance was evaluated using Pearson correlation coefficient (<i>r</i>), and error metrics (RMSE, MAE, and MedAE), based on cross-validation and independent test set validation. Predicted WHO-FC values were subsequently linked to externally validated, stratified real-world health care cost estimates to approximate future economic burden. <strong>Results:</strong> Among the evaluated models, k-nearest neighbors showed the highest predictive performance (<i>r</i>=0.40; <i>R</i><sup>2</sup>=0.16), followed by LASSO regression (<i>r</i>=0.30; <i>R</i><sup>2</sup>=0.09). Prediction errors were comparable across models. Estimated mean health care costs ranged from US $12,481 to $12,614 per patient per month. Despite a significant improvement in WHO-FC over 12 months, estimated health care costs remained largely unchanged, reflecting persistent economic burden across functional class strata. <strong>Conclusions:</strong> This study demonstrates the feasibility of an ML-based framework that integrates prediction of functional status with health care cost estimation in PAH. While predictive performance was moderate, the approach illustrates how ML models can be extended beyond clinical end points toward system-level applications such as cost forecasting and resource planning. Further validation in larger, multicenter cohorts is required before clinical or health system implementation.

### [Concept-Aware Learning Framework for Detecting Common Symptoms in Social Media Text: Model Development and Evaluation Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e94769)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-15` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `63` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e94769`

<strong>Background:</strong> Social media offers valuable information for public health surveillance, but detecting valid symptom expressions in noisy and informal text remains challenging. Fine-tuned language models can achieve strong test accuracy while relying on spurious patterns, limiting their reliability in real-world applications. <strong>Objective:</strong> This study aimed to develop a more robust symptom detection model by incorporating surveillance-relevant attributes into the training objective and introducing a counterfactual-based evaluation framework to assess attribute sensitivity. <strong>Methods:</strong> We proposed a concept-aware learning framework (CALF), which combines multiattribute counterfactual data augmentation with multitask learning. We compared standard fine-tuning (baseline) with data-level interventions, a model-level intervention, hybrid strategies, and prompt-based methods across multiple encoders and LLMs for data augmentation. Performance was evaluated on the following 3 datasets: the MedWeb test using macro <i>F</i><sub>1</sub>-score (m-<i>F</i><sub>1</sub>), the MedWeb checklist using flip accuracy (FA), and a supplementary test set using <i>F</i><sub>1</sub>-score for flu-or-fever detection (f-<i>F</i><sub>1</sub>). FA measures sensitivity to surveillance-relevant attributes, and the supplementary test score serves as an indicator for out-of-distribution performance. <strong>Results:</strong> The baseline achieved an m-<i>F</i><sub>1</sub>-score of 0.871 on the test set but a low overall FA on the checklist (FA=0.342). It also has limited sensitivity to recovery status, timing of occurrence, and uncertainty of expression. Compared with the baseline, CALF has a comparable MedWeb test score (m-<i>F</i><sub>1</sub>=0.880 vs 0.871; <i>P</i>=.18), but a substantially higher FA (FA=0.830 vs 0.342; <i>P</i>=.001) and supplementary test score (f-<i>F</i><sub>1</sub>=0.856 vs 0.797; <i>P</i>=.007). CALF also improved sensitivity to recovery, timing, and uncertainty across pretrained encoders. The gains were generally larger when GPT-4.1 (OpenAI) was used for data augmentation. Under GPT-4.1, CALF often achieved the highest overall FA among encoder-based methods. When Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct (Alibaba Cloud) was used, an alternative hybrid strategy may achieve comparable or slightly better results. Checklist performance was strongly associated with supplementary test performance (ρ=0.811), whereas MedWeb test performance showed weak association with supplementary test performance (ρ=–0.042). Prompting GPT-4.1 achieved the highest FA overall (FA=0.920) and the highest supplementary test score (f-<i>F</i><sub>1</sub>=0.910), but a lower test set score than the baseline (m-<i>F</i><sub>1</sub>=0.776 vs 0.871). <strong>Conclusions:</strong> Standard held-out metrics alone do not adequately characterize robustness to dataset-specific or spuriously correlated features. Models may perform well on the test set while remaining insensitive to attributes that influence whether a symptom mention is relevant to surveillance. The MedWeb checklist complements conventional evaluation by measuring sensitivity to controlled attribute changes and may indicate out-of-distribution performance. CALF improved sensitivity to surveillance-relevant attributes while maintaining strong classification performance, supporting concept-aware learning as a practical strategy for developing more robust encoder-based models.

### [Construction of an AI-Assisted Pedigree Mapping System for Hereditary Gynecological Tumors: Algorithm Development and Pilot Validation Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e78080)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-15` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `49` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e78080`

Background: Pedigree mapping serves as the first step in genetic counseling. Traditional methods relying on manual pedigree drawing and information collection are inefficient and prone to errors. Objective: This study aimed to design an intelligent pedigree mapping system based on a specialized model, intended to automate family history collection and standardized pedigree map generation through conversational interaction, thereby enhancing the clinical efficiency of genetic counseling. Methods: This study adopted a participatory design and iterative development approach. We formed an interdisciplinary team comprising gynecologic oncologists, genetic counselors, AI engineers, and patient representatives. The system was built around a proprietary, specialized large language model for gynecologic oncology, featuring a microservices architecture that included pedigree data collection. Through 4 iterative cycles, we integrated specialized knowledge bases using retrieval-augmented generation technology and applied a novel graph-based kinship inference and validation module. The system’s performance was comprehensively evaluated based on the accuracy of pedigree map generation and user feedback on usability. Results: We significantly improved system performance by optimizing dialogue strategies, expanding the knowledge base, and applying self-developed algorithms to improve data extraction accuracy. The initial database of the system defined 100 core information nodes and matched approximately 800 user expression examples, while the final pilot version of the database was expanded to 245 core information nodes and 2256 user expression examples. Although the AI function was limited in the early stage, the final version of the system demonstrated outstanding accuracy in generating genealogy nodes and relationships. Test users generally gave positive reviews of the system’s innovation, ease of use, and intuitiveness of information presentation, believing it to have great potential in clinical practice. Conclusions: The developed system is an accurate, efficient auxiliary tool, and pilot validation showed promising preliminary performance. After pilot clinical validation, it holds promise for integration into routine clinical workflows to optimize genetic counseling services.

### [Digital Campaign to Promote COVID-19 Vaccination and Clinical Trial Participation Among Vietnamese Americans: Observational Engagement Study](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e90301)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-10-15` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `54` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e90301`

Background: The COVID-19 pandemic necessitated innovative approaches to health communication, particularly for non–English-speaking communities. Vietnamese Americans, who report lower rates of English proficiency, faced unique challenges accessing facts and subsequent “infodemic” of rapidly spreading information. Objective: This study aims to evaluate the feasibility and engagement patterns of a community-engaged bilingual digital campaign delivered through Google’s Display &amp; Video 360 (DV360) platform to Vietnamese Americans in Texas, comparing performance across multiple advertising formats. Methods: We implemented a 2-phase digital campaign on (DV360): static display advertisements (Phase 1: February 15-March 31, 2022) and audiovisual content (Phase 2: May 26-June 30, 2022). With community-based organizations (CBOs), 6 static displays, 2 videos, and 2 audio advertisements were developed in English and Vietnamese using focus group review and back-translation. Advertisement placements were constrained to a Texas Vietnamese audience segment. Total budget was US $9,472.54. Performance was evaluated using impressions, click-through rate (CTR), cost metrics, cost per click (CPC), cost per thousand impressions (CPM), and cost per view (CPV), with Wilson 95% CI, and two-proportion z-tests for substantive comparisons. Results: The campaign generated 4,974,357 impressions, reaching approximately 1.4 million unique users in Phase 1, and 105,000 in Phase 2. Vietnamese-language display advertisements achieved a higher CTR than English-language advertisements (0.105% vs 0.046%; ²=480.6; &lt;.001; risk ratio 2.29). Mobile placements outperformed desktop within each language (Vietnamese mobile 0.156% vs desktop 0.045%; English mobile 0.064% vs desktop 0.019%). Audio CTR was 1.911% in May but fell to 0.119% in June on small denominators (≈1800 impressions; 5 clicks). Impressions concentrated in expected Texas metropolitan areas (Houston 24.4%, Dallas 19.9%, Austin 7.9%). Phase 2 Google Analytics Behavior Flow showed 55.2% of language-distinguished landings were on Vietnamese pages (²=6.7; =.01). Cost efficiency was favorable (CPC US $2.35 and CPM US $1.45). Conclusions: A community-engaged bilingual digital campaign produced measurable in-language engagement among Vietnamese American audiences in Texas. Like other COVID-19 outreach campaigns directed at historically marginalized groups, researchers worked closely with CBOs, paying particular attention to messaging, language translation, and graphic representation in display, audio, and video advertisements to reach the population of interest. What differed from other campaigns was the use of DV360 allowing dissemination across multiple platforms and devices. Results are limited to engagement because direct impact on COVID-19 immunization or participation in clinical trials was not measured. Future directions can explore more clinically significant outcomes, in-language radio advertising, and harnessing AI to develop and monitor public health campaigns. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/02fb9907ad7ea541a22be191e314f7a9" />

### [Implementing Taiwan National Health Insurance Quality Indicators as Clinical Quality Language on Fast Healthcare Interoperability Resources: Development and Feasibility Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e108093)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-15` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `62` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e108093`

<strong>Background:</strong> Clinical Quality Language (CQL) and Fast Healthcare Interoperability Resources (FHIR) support executable electronic clinical quality measures. Translating national claims–based indicators into CQL remains challenging because terminology, exclusions, and data semantics require adaptation. To our knowledge, Taiwan National Health Insurance (NHI) medical-center indicators have not previously been represented as an executable CQL library. <strong>Objective:</strong> This study aimed to develop CQL libraries derived from 39 Taiwan NHI medical-center quality indicator codes and evaluate technical executability and computational consistency using synthetic data and real-world cross-institution medication records. <strong>Methods:</strong> We implemented 39 CQL libraries across 6 principal FHIR R4 clinical resources using 21 data elements, with documented adaptations where official requirements could not be represented. Compiled Expression Logical Model was executed against author-defined synthetic fixtures. In a retrospective 2-campus study, a separately coded Python implementation and the CQL pipeline computed aggregate numerators and denominators for 20 indicators from the same deidentified records, represented as a long table and FHIR resources, respectively. <strong>Results:</strong> All 39 CQL libraries compiled and executed against the predefined synthetic fixtures, and their outputs matched the author-defined expected results (39/39 libraries; 136/136 early-refill grace-period control groups). The real-world dataset included 88 patients (Taipei: n=65; Linkou: n=23), 1597 medication records, and 630 visits from January 1 to June 2, 2026. Across the 20 medication-based indicators, the 2 pipelines produced identical aggregate numerators and denominators (20/20), and 7 of the 8 cross-institution drug-class overlap indicators had nonzero numerators. All 2317 FHIR resources passed base FHIR R4 structural validation, and Anatomical Therapeutic Chemical mapping coverage was 98.3% (1570/1597 records). <strong>Conclusions:</strong> The implemented NHI-derived indicator logic was executable and computationally consistent across synthetic testing and aggregate real-world comparisons. These findings do not establish complete fidelity to official NHI specifications. Further evaluation requires expert semantic review, patient-level testing, and execution across independent CQL/FHIR environments. <strong>Trial Registration:</strong>

### [Improving Musculoskeletal Care Quality With a Web-Based Clinical Decision Support System in UK Primary Care: Mixed Methods Cluster-Randomized Controlled Trial](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e89734)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-10-15` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `63` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e89734`

Background: Musculoskeletal (MSK) conditions account for one in seven general practice consultations in the United Kingdom. Despite national guidelines, suboptimal care remains common. Digital clinical decision support systems (CDSSs) offer a promising route to improve care quality but have not been robustly evaluated in MSK primary care. Objective: This study aimed to evaluate the efficacy, clinical effectiveness and cost-effectiveness, carbon reduction, safety, and implementation of a CDSS (CrossCover) for MSK consultations in UK general practice. Methods: We conducted a mixed methods, cluster-randomized controlled trial in 19 general practices between February and July 2024. Practices were randomized 1:1, stratified by list size. Intervention practices received training and access to the web-based CDSS for 3 months. We analyzed anonymized electronic health records to identify all adult MSK consultations. The primary outcome was the proportion of patient records containing one or more of 12 predefined non–evidence-based management options, assessed by blinded clinical review of 120 patient records (60 per arm). Reviewers assessed the complete 3-month record in both arms; in the intervention arm, suboptimal management was counted only where it occurred at a consultation in which the CDSS was used. Secondary outcomes included intention-to-treat treatment comparisons of self-management advice, prescriptions, referrals, and imaging. Clinician engagement, safety, environmental and economic modeling, and clinician experiences from semistructured interviews were evaluated. Outcome assessors were blinded; clinicians were not. Results: Among 10,182 eligible patients with MSK conditions (4268 intervention; 5914 control), the CDSS was used in 365 (8.6%) patient cases. Intention-to-treat analysis identified that the intervention increased self-management alone. Per-protocol analysis demonstrated that when used, suboptimal care arose in 3% (2/60) of CDSS-guided consultations vs 25% (15/60) of control consultations (adjusted odds ratio 12.30, 95% CI 2.52‐59.9; =.002). It also increased self-management advice and community referrals, and it reduced prescribing and fit-note use. Safety concerns were logged in 6.6% (24/365) of uses with no high-risk issues identified. Environmental modeling estimated an 18% reduction in carbon emissions and 10% annual cost savings if the intervention were scaled. Interviews (n=18) highlighted positive perceptions of the CDSS’s content and safety netting but noted challenges in integration. Conclusions: When used, the CrossCover CDSS significantly reduced suboptimal primary care MSK management. However, while safety and clinician feedback were favorable, overall engagement was limited, underscoring the need for improved digital integration, workflow alignment, and implementation support. Trial Registration: ISRCTN Registry ISRCTN38924614; https://www.isrctn.com/ISRCTN38924614 <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/105c7d7e27d8de0b5086bcb99dbd07da" />

### [Labcorp to buy BioReference assets for up to $872M](https://www.medtechdive.com/news/labcorp-to-buy-bioreference-assets-for-up-to-872m/833116/)

Labcorp has agreed to buy certain laboratory testing businesses from Opko Health subsidiary BioReference Health for $722 million, plus an additional sum of up to $150 million tied to revenue from a prostate cancer blood test. The final purchase price could be adjusted based on closing revenues, the companies said Thursday. Labcorp is acquiring businesses focused on clinical and women's health testing services, urology and diagnostic services provided to correctional institutions. The deal also adds more than 80 patient service centers to Labcorp’s network. In addition, the lab services provider is gaining BioReference Health's 4Kscore test, which has Food and Drug Administration approval to help predict the likelihood of finding aggressive prostate cancer before a biopsy decision.

### [Large Language Model–Assisted Review of Clinical Trial Informed Consent for Data Reuse: Retrieval-Augmented Generation Approach](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e95417)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-15` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `64` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e95417`

Background: Regulatory frameworks such as the Belmont Report, the Common Rule, and the Declaration of Helsinki require informed consent to ensure participants understand a study’s purpose and can make voluntary decisions about their involvement. Regulations, including the General Data Protection Regulation (Regulation [EU] 2016/679), further emphasize that consent must be freely given and revocable without disadvantage. Although informed consent forms (ICFs) are intended to be clear and accessible, they have become increasingly lengthy and complex. Large language models (LLMs) offer the potential to navigate and interpret this complexity and have shown promise in biomedical information extraction tasks. However, their susceptibility to hallucinations limits their reliability in high-stakes settings. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can mitigate such errors. Objective: This study evaluates the integration of GPT-4.1 with RAG for reviewing data reuse language in ICFs and its ability to interpret complex ICF language. Methods: First, we processed 438 ICFs from different trials, including multiple countries, languages, and versions of ICFs. Using expertly curated prompts, we extracted information about data reuse using GPT-4.1. By comparing the LLM-generated data reuse outputs with human expert ground truth, we evaluated accuracy and the time required to extract information for each ICF. To further validate the workflow, we evaluated an independent set of 488 ICFs spanning additional trials, languages, and regions. During the validation phase, the workflow was evaluated using a baseline assessment (89 ICFs) followed by 3 iterative optimization rounds assessed on 14, 281, and 104 ICFs, respectively. For this cohort, we assessed the correctness of LLM outputs along with the quality of supporting evidence provided by the model. Results: Across the initial set of 438 ICFs, the system achieved 81.6% accuracy. During validation, the baseline accuracy using the preoptimized prompts was 84.0% (89 documents). Accuracy improved sequentially across the 3 iterative optimization rounds, increasing from 82.3% in the first round (14 documents) to 90.7% in the second round (281 documents), and reaching 92.1% in the third and final round on an independent holdout cohort of 104 documents. Ultimately, using our RAG-based approach, the system accurately extracted data reuse information across multiple languages. Conclusions: Our RAG-based workflow demonstrated the ability to effectively support the ICF review process for data reuse and interpret complex consent language, contributing to improved consistency in ICF evaluations for data reuse.

### [NEJM study supports CGM use for people with diabetes who don’t take insulin](https://www.medtechdive.com/news/nejm-study-supports-cgm-use-for-people-with-diabetes-who-dont-take-insulin/833124/)

Results of a Dexcom-funded study published in the New England Journal of Medicine Wednesday demonstrated that people with Type 2 diabetes who don’t take insulin may benefit from wearing a continuous glucose monitor. Dexcom first shared the results at the American Diabetes Association’s Scientific Sessions this summer. The study found that people who wore a CGM had a larger reduction in hemoglobin A1c than people who received routine care after 26 weeks. The published results come as diabetes tech firms anticipate the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services to expand Medicare coverage of CGMs for people with diabetes who don’t take insulin. Having a peer-reviewed publication in a leading medical journal “should be a catalyst” for a CMS decision, William Blair analysts wrote in a research note.

### [Open-Source Anatomical Illustration for Digital Back Pain Documentation: Development and Feasibility Evaluation](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e75737)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-15` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `46` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e75737`

Background: Accurate documentation of back pain is essential for effective diagnosis and treatment; however, conventional paper-based methods often lack precision and standardization, limiting their clinical utility. Digital pain drawings can improve consistency and integration; however, the literature continues to report variability in digital pain drawing methods, quantification approaches, body schemas, and implementation. Moreover, open access, anatomically labeled vector assets mapped to clinical terminology remain limited. Objective: This study aimed to develop an anatomically accurate, digitally segmented, open-source vector illustration of a sex-neutral human body (front and back views) for standardized back pain documentation and conduct a user-centered design validation and feasibility evaluation of its perceived usefulness, communication value, and visual acceptability. Methods: An interdisciplinary team used an iterative methodology informed by standard anatomical references to create the illustration, emphasizing anatomical precision, digital compatibility, and interoperability with medical coding systems. A survey-based design validation study (N=298) was conducted in the United Kingdom through the Prolific platform to evaluate perceived usefulness, confidence in identifying hypothetical pain regions (without an interactive drawing task or comparator), aesthetic appeal, and suggestions for improvement. Quantitative results were summarized descriptively with 95% CIs, and qualitative feedback guided final refinements. The finalized illustration was released under a CC BY 4.0 license. Results: Mean confidence ratings were 5.7 out of 7 (SD 1.2) for indicating pain regions using the illustration (95% CI 5.5-5.8) and 5.6 out of 7 (SD 1.2) for communicating pain with health care professionals (95% CI 5.5-5.8). Aesthetic ratings were more mixed (mean 4.3 out of 7, SD 1.5; 95% CI 4.1-4.5). User feedback highlighted the need for improved visual clarity, leading to adjustments in color contrast and anatomical proportions. Aesthetic concerns, including the removal of the areolae and changes to the facial features, were also addressed to enhance user comfort and neutrality. Conclusions: The resulting anatomically accurate vector illustration provides a feasible, open-source foundation for digital back pain documentation tools and may support patient-clinician communication and integration with electronic health records. The design validation study supports acceptability and perceived usefulness, but it does not establish superiority over existing body maps or objective accuracy of pain localization. By releasing it under a CC BY 4.0 license, we encourage broad adoption and community-driven improvements.

### [Optimizing Clinical Temporal Relation Extraction With Large Language Models: Comparative Analysis](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e89957)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-15` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `62` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e89957`

Background: Clinical temporal relation extraction (CTRE) is essential for reconstructing patient timelines from unstructured electronic health records (EHRs). However, the linguistic complexity of clinical notes and the high cost of expert annotation impede the development of large-scale training corpora. While large language models (LLMs) have transformed general natural language processing, their application to CTRE remains underexplored. Objective: This study aimed to determine the optimal adaptation strategy for CTRE by conducting a controlled comparative benchmarking of LLM architectures and fine-tuning methodologies in both data-rich and limited-data regimes. Methods: We evaluated 4 LLMs representing 2 distinct architectures—transformer encoders (GatorTron [University of Florida and NVIDIA]) and transformer decoders (LLaMA 3.1-8B [Meta AI] and MeLLaMA-13B [Yale University and University of Florida]). We compared four adaptation strategies: (1) standard fine-tuning, (2) hard-prompting, (3) soft-prompting, and (4) low-rank adaptation (LoRA). Experiments were conducted on the 2012 i2b2 CTRE benchmark in both full-supervision and 1-shot scenarios. Results: Recent LLMs combined with appropriate adaptation strategies achieved strong performance for CTRE on the 2012 i2b2 benchmark. In the data-rich setting, the best-performing configuration was LLaMA3.1-8B with quantized low-rank adaptation (QLoRA) applied to the query and value projection layers, achieving a micro--score of 89.15% (0.30). Comparative analyses showed that QLoRA applied only to the query and value projection layers achieved performance comparable to QLoRA applied to all linear layers, while requiring fewer trainable parameters. Hard-prompting showed particular effectiveness in the 1-shot scenario compared with standard fine-tuning for GatorTron-Base. Overall, Decoder-based models generally outperformed encoder-based models under QLoRA-based adaptation. However, under the most resource-constrained setting, where both training data and trainable parameters were highly limited, encoder-based models achieved better performance than decoder-based models. Conclusions: This study provides a controlled benchmark for adapting LLMs to CTRE under different data and parameter constraints. Our findings suggest that QLoRA targeting only the query and value projection layers is a computationally efficient and competitive adaptation strategy. Decoder-based LLMs generally achieved stronger performance under QLoRA-based adaptation, whereas domain-specific encoder-based models showed advantages under the most resource-constrained soft-prompting setting. These results highlight the importance of selecting adaptation strategies according to both model architecture and available computational resources.

### [Same Name, Moving Target: Model Version Drift in Syntheses of Large Language Models for Mental Health](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e111307/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-10-15` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `62` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e111307/`

This letter comments on the systematic review and meta-analysis of large language models (LLMs) in mental health care by Leung and colleagues (JMIR AI 2026;5:e87730). The bivariate meta-analysis with Hartung-Knapp adjustments is methodologically sound, and its cautious conclusions are warranted. I raise an unaddressed threat to the pooled estimates: model version drift. Over the search window (November 2022 to March 2026), &quot;GPT-4&quot; did not denote a single intervention; released in March 2023, it was followed by API snapshots whose behavior differed materially, with accuracy on a prime-number identification task falling from 84% to 51% between the March and June 2023 versions. Because the review extracted only model name and model type and did not capture version or testing dates, the estimates pooled under GPT-4 (k=4) and GPT-3.5 (k=4) may each span several substantively different models, and the pooled GPT-4 estimate may correspond to no single system a clinician could deploy. This concern is distinct from the acknowledged limitation that few studies evaluated the newest variants, which concerns currency rather than comparability. I suggest reporting model version and testing date for each pooled study (consistent with TRIPOD-LLM item 6a), examining robustness to version mixing in sensitivity analyses, and qualifying the abstract comparative statements accordingly. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/e770a7e6754928129106feabee847a04" />

### [The Forgotten Student—Generative AI Use in Medical Education and the Technology-Integrated Educational Design (TIED) Road Map: Scoping Review](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e96099)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-10-15` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `59` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e96099`

Background: The growing impact of generative AI (GenAI) on medical education has given rise to many reviews discussing AI literacy, trustworthiness, potential uses, and institutional or faculty benefits. However, there has been surprisingly little review-level attention to its impact on students and learning. Objective: This scoping review examines the impact of GenAI-enabled educational interventions on undergraduate medical students. Methods: We followed the PRISMA-ScR (Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses extension for Scoping Reviews) guidelines. An electronic search was conducted between September 22 and September 24, 2025, across Ovid (MEDLINE and Embase), PubMed, Web of Science, and Scopus, with no language or date restrictions, and excluding reviews, commentaries, letters, editorials, preprints, conference papers, and study protocols. Each record was independently screened by two reviewers, who thoroughly discussed and agreed upon its final inclusion in the analysis. We used a modified version of the Kirkpatrick model as an analytical framework for the final dataset. Results: Twenty-seven studies were included. Most were published in 2025 (n=21) and in the or its sister journals (n=9). The educational interventions described included GenAI used for simulated patient experiences, knowledge or decision support, and content generation. The majority of studies fell under Kirkpatrick level 1 (n=23), focusing on initial user reactions. The studies that fell under Kirkpatrick level 2 (n=16) examined learning outcomes in terms of knowledge gain, skill acquisition, and learning attitudes. No studies were classified as Kirkpatrick level 3. Three studies demonstrated explicit theoretical grounding and therefore were classified as Kirkpatrick level 4 in our modified model. Only 13 studies spanned across levels. Numerous studies fell outside the model, including studies without student-facing interventions (119 studies) and studies focused on GenAI performance (110 studies). Although these studies were not included in the final analysis, they revealed the extent to which other topics dominate the academic conversation on GenAI in medical education. Conclusions: Our review highlights the volume of studies that prioritize tool performance over learning processes, as well as a trend toward the fragmentation of educational design elements in research evaluating GenAI-enabled educational interventions. Therefore, based on our findings, we developed the Technology-Integrated Educational Design (TIED) road map that reimagines the Kirkpatrick model as a tool to guide student-centered, theory-driven, and iterative design of technology-enabled educational interventions. By integrating preinstructional exploration, mechanism-oriented thinking, educational design, implementation, and evaluation within a single student-centered framework, the road map encourages educators and researchers to consider not only whether technology-enabled interventions work and are acceptable, but also how, for whom, and under what conditions they support meaningful learning. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/7bfe6fbef02da250f038415934a4b336" />

### [Toward a Living Meta-Framework for Evaluating Generative AI in Mental Health](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e89211/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-10-15` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `41` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e89211/`

The proliferation of evaluative approaches for generative AI tools used for mental health purposes reflects much-needed attention to safety, ethics, and emerging risks. However, limited alignment across frameworks complicates comparisons, slows progress toward shared goals, and provides little clarity for regulatory processes. The result is a fragmented field that lacks cohesive guidance as the technology and its real-world patterns of use continue to evolve. This position paper synthesizes current evaluative approaches, outlines sources of divergence across existing frameworks, and identifies shared domains that could serve as anchors for coordinated development. We advocate for a living, flexible meta-framework maintained by a global, cross-sector consortium that can harmonize criteria, support consistent evaluation across jurisdictions, and adapt alongside scientific, clinical, and technological advances. Such a structure can strengthen safety, transparency, and methodological consistency while giving researchers, clinicians, developers, and policymakers a shared foundation for assessing generative AI tools in mental health care. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/601575317a639ad96e383bd445d779a0" />

### [AI for Poststroke Epilepsy: Scoping Review of Imaging-Based Prediction Models and Implications for African Settings](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e96828/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-10-16` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `56` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e96828/`

Background: Poststroke seizures (PSS) and poststroke epilepsy (PSE) are important neurological complications associated with poorer functional recovery, increased mortality, and reduced quality of life after stroke. Neuroimaging plays a central role in identifying patients at increased risk, and recent advances in AI have enabled the development of imaging-based prediction models that may improve individualized risk stratification. However, the current evidence remains fragmented, and the extent to which these approaches are applicable to African health care settings is unclear. Objective: This scoping review aimed to chart the existing global evidence on the development of imaging-based prediction models that can help in predicting PSS and PSE, especially AI-based ones. The purpose was to look into the imaging techniques, computational algorithms, and validation methodologies used and the representation of African populations. Methods: This scoping review was performed using the Joanna Briggs Institute methodological framework, and the results were presented based on the Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses Extension for Scoping Reviews (PRISMA-ScR) framework. Articles searched using databases such as PubMed, Embase, Scopus, Web of Science, IEEE Xplore, and Google Scholar involved the application of computed tomography (CT), magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), angiography imaging techniques, radiomic markers, or multimodal neuroimaging techniques in statistical and AI-based prediction models for seizure-related outcomes after stroke. Results: Twenty-three studies published between 2005 and 2026 met the eligibility criteria. The evidence demonstrated a clear methodological evolution from conventional imaging-based prediction models to radiomics, machine learning, deep learning, explainable AI, and multimodal approaches. MRI and CT were the predominant imaging modalities, with cortical involvement, lesion location, infarct volume, hemorrhagic transformation, and quantitative radiomic features emerging as the most frequently reported imaging predictors. Most AI models relied on internal validation, while relatively few underwent external validation. The evidence base was concentrated in East Asia, Europe, and North America, with no eligible imaging-based AI models having been developed or externally validated using African datasets. Conclusions: Imaging-based AI for predicting PSS and PSE has progressed rapidly, but important methodological and geographical gaps remain. Limited external validation, heterogeneous reporting, and the absence of African development and validation cohorts restrict confidence in the generalizability of existing models. Future research should prioritize the development of explainable imaging-based AI models using representative African neuroimaging datasets and their external validation in multicenter studies. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/9e5693948385697cd77de32218793a32" />

### [AMA releases governance framework for agentic AI in healthcare](https://www.medtechdive.com/news/ama-releases-governance-framework-for-agentic-ai-in-healthcare/833208/)
